Populations carry simulation error, and biology dictates which conformations are deal-breakers, so a candidate that fails one is disqualified, not merely ranked lower. No standard rule captures this. We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. Together, these define a family of targets, amortised by a single conditional GFlowNet. Pricing the tail rather than averaging moves 2-10 times more mass to candidates that pass every conformation. One network covers the family to within 0.37-2.7x the error of a perfect sampler. An exact-KL oracle, a copy trained on the true target, shows if a shortfall is the optimiser's or the architecture's. | **[Dynamics-inspired Structure Hallucination for Protein-protein Interaction Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06214v1)** | 2026-01-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-protein interaction (PPI) represents a central challenge within the biology field, and accurately predicting the consequences of mutations in this context is crucial for drug design and protein engineering. Secondly, PPI takes place dynamically, which is rarely integrated into the DL architecture design. To address these obstacles, we present a novel framework named Refine-PPI with two key enhancements. First, we introduce a structure refinement module trained by a mask mutation modeling (MMM) task on available wild-type structures, which is then transferred to produce the inaccessible mutant structures. Second, we employ a new kind of geometric network, called the probability density cloud network (PDC-Net), to capture 3D dynamic variations and encode the atomic uncertainty associated with PPI. We present in this work the first comprehensive experimental analysis of this advantage of QGANS applied to SAT4 image generation, obtaining an exponential advantage in capacity scaling for a quantum generator in the hybrid latent style-based QGAN architecture. Careful tuning of the autoencoder is crucial to obtain stable, reliable results. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. ## multi-objective optimization

| **[A Game-Theoretic Decision Framework for Optimal Selection of Coordination Detection Methods in Multi-UAV Fleet Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02383v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Detecting coordination among unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) fleets operating in shared airspace and identifying the route-lead aircraft whose navigation decisions govern fleet behavior presents a fundamental speed--accuracy trade-off: fast methods enable real-time traffic management but sacrifice detection fidelity, while accurate methods may exceed the time budget for actionable airspace deconfliction. This paper presents a game-theoretic decision framework that resolves this trade-off by formulating method selection as a two-player zero-sum game between a Monitor (selecting computational methods and parameters) and Nature (selecting the unknown traffic scenario). Experimental evaluation across 200 randomized configurations spanning 5--50 aircraft demonstrates that the framework recommends distinct method portfolios depending on operational priority: Koopman Phase dominates balanced (70.6%) and speed-priority (79.7%) profiles, while CRQA emerges as primary (47.4%) when route-lead identification is prioritized. The framework achieves a guaranteed game value of 0.29--0.53 (normalized utility) across all tested preference profiles, providing the first principled, scenario-adaptive methodology for computational method selection in UTM fleet monitoring operations.</p></details> |  |

The resulting motions better preserve task-relevant interactions and provide reference trajectories that can be tracked more reliably than those produced by widely used retargeting methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[NeuroPareto: Calibrated Acquisition for Costly Many-Goal Search in Vast Parameter Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03901v7)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The pursuit of optimal trade-offs in high-dimensional search spaces under stringent computational constraints poses a fundamental challenge for contemporary multi-objective optimization. To address this gap, we propose EvoPS (Evolutionary Patch Selection), a novel framework that formulates patch selection as a multi-objective optimization problem and leverages an evolutionary search to simultaneously minimize the number of selected patch embeddings and maximize the performance of a downstream similarity search task, generating a Pareto front of optimal trade-off solutions. | **[Instant Software Factories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36254v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can quickly build prototypes, but a long tail of fixes, optimizations, tests, and releases separates a prototype from a product. | **[KubePACS: Kubernetes Cluster Using Performant, Highly Available, and Cost Efficient Spot Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24027v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud users aim to minimize cost while maximizing performance by selecting the most suitable instance types for their workloads. | **[Tradeoff-Aware Territory Design: A Multi-Objective Decision System for Enterprise Customer-to-Seller Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31701v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer journeys are shaped not only by product experiences and marketing, but also by recurring resource-allocation decisions. | **[SliMOO: Interpretable Multi-Objective Evolutionary Search for LLM Depth Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27812v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) grow more powerful, their scale makes them costly to serve. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. | **[Jacobian Descent for Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16232v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many optimization problems require balancing multiple conflicting objectives. This algorithm iteratively updates parameters using the Jacobian matrix of a vector-valued objective function, in which each row is the gradient of an individual objective. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 14 figures, Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)</p></details> |
| **[Communication-Efficient Federated Domain Generalization in Edge Networks via Dynamic Prototype Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. GCAM operates within the low-dimensional feature manifold to dynamically resolve multi-objective optimization conflicts with minimal edge computation latency, thereby reducing computation-induced network outages. Experiments demonstrate that DPGR-GCAM improves cross-domain generalization and sustains convergence under network fading scenarios with a 10\% client participation rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Python Tool for Visualizing Variability of Pareto Fronts over Multiple Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08852v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperparameter optimization is crucial to achieving high performance in deep learning. We, therefore, develop a Python package for this purpose and describe the usage. The package is available at https://github.com/nabenabe0928/empirical-attainment-func. | **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. We then present a simulation study using an LLM-based DM agent and benchmark ParetoChat against interactive methods implemented in DESDEO, including reference-point and NIMBUS methods. An application to historical courier-order assignment instances illustrates how users can explore objective extremes and intermediate trade-offs through natural-language requests. The results indicate that LLM agents may provide a practical interaction layer between business stakeholders and optimization tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. When renovation is delayed beyond the infrastructure's remaining lifespan, costly emergency interventions become necessary to prevent failure. Decision makers must therefore balance expected emergency intervention costs against traffic congestion impacts. We formalize this trade-off as a road network maintenance scheduling problem with uncertain deadlines, which presents optimization challenges including computationally expensive evaluation and an exponentially growing solution space. We formulate the problem as a bi-level multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly accounts for uncertain infrastructure lifespans through probabilistic failure models. We develop a progressive lower bound evaluation method: candidate schedules receive quick, optimistic estimates of their traffic impact from machine learning based surrogate models, and these estimates are refined by exact traffic simulation only for promising candidates, so that full evaluation is reserved almost exclusively for schedules that can advance the Pareto front. Embedded in a multi-objective genetic algorithm, this evaluation strategy improves solution quality by enabling many more iterations within a fixed computational budget. We demonstrate the method's effectiveness and scalability on substantially larger problem instances than previously addressed in the literature: 76 and 80 projects on the Sioux Falls and Anaheim networks, respectively. Under an identical 24-hour computational budget across 30 random seeds, the method completes up to 87 times as many optimization iterations as standard NSGA-II and achieves statistically significant improvements across multiple performance metrics over both standard NSGA-II and a surrogate-assisted baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures. Accepted manuscript of the article published in Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cacaie.2026.100174</p></details> |
| **[In-Context Preference-Conditioned Pareto Set Learning from Limited Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21736v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization involves diverse preferences over conflicting objectives. Rather than returning a finite set of trade-off solutions, preference-conditioned Pareto set learning seeks to map a specified preference directly to a corresponding Pareto optimal solution. Existing methods either optimize separately for each preference or train a problem-specific Pareto solution model, requiring repeated search across preferences or repeated training across problems. We introduce in-context preference-conditioned Pareto set learning (ICPSL), a framework that amortizes both preference-wise solution search and problem-specific model construction. ICPSL pretrains a foundation-style hypernetwork on a large and diverse set of synthetic prior data. Given the offline observations of an unseen problem, the pretrained hypernetwork learns to generate a problem-specific, preference-conditioned Pareto solution model in a single forward pass. The generated model can then be reused to predict solutions for arbitrary preferences without problem-specific training or preference-wise search. Experiments on synthetic and real-world problems show that ICPSL produces high-quality Pareto solutions while providing substantial speedups in problem-specific solution-model construction.</p></details> |  |
| **[First Mathematical Runtime Analyses of Evolutionary Multitask Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21311v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To facilitate the solving of complex problems, evolutionary multitask optimization is regarded as a promising technique due to its capacity for cross-task knowledge transfer. We analyze how the representative multiobjective multifactorial evolutionary algorithm (MO-MFEA) covers the Pareto fronts of two bi-objective problems simultaneously. | **[When the implementation matters more than the algorithm: NSGA-II and other multi-objective optimizers in engineering design tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22397v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering design tools increasingly embed optimizers as ready-made components that users select by algorithm name, while operator settings and bound, integer and duplicate handling stay fixed inside each tool. Eighteen configurations were run at 5,000 evaluations under three population allocations, with statistics fixed in advance. Four NSGA-II implementations differed by up to 5% in hypervolume, more than three canonical algorithms of different families differed from one another, and their response to population size differed by up to 10 percentage points; for two MOEA/D implementations, by 28. On ZDT test functions the same NSGA-II implementations differed up to 470-fold in IGD. An ablation in one open-source NSGA-II reproduced the behaviour of one tool from its documented settings but not the gaps of the other two, so documented parameters do not identify an implementation. Counting the budget in wall-clock time reversed the ranking of the model-based optimizer, and reporting the final population instead of the archive understated some configurations by up to a third; most differences fell below the surrogate's error. | **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. Contemporary methods mitigate this challenge by exploiting pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for their learned transferable features. Some recent ones augment the scarce data by retrieving relevant yet out-of-distribution (OOD) examples from external data sources. However, they largely overlook an equally fundamental challenge: the absence of validation data for model selection and hyperparameter tuning. As a result, the adapted VLMs overfit the few-shot training data and generalize poorly to OOD test data. We show that retrieved data provide a natural source of validation, but their direct use creates a fundamental paradox. Since the retrieved samples are inherently OOD w.r.t the few-shot ID training data, adapting a VLM to the ID data constantly degrades its performance on the retrieved data. Consequently, conventional validation relying on the accuracy of retrieved data favors no adaptation and prevents improved generalization. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. This strategy enables hyperparameter tuning, which had been unrealistically done by using test data, thereby mitigating overfitting and improving generalization. We integrate it into a stage-wise finetuning pipeline, termed Validation-Enabled Stage-wise Tuning (VEST). Extensive experiments on five established benchmarks show that VEST consistently outperforms existing approaches on both ID and OOD test data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://hannawang09.github.io/projects/vest/</p></details> |
| **[Large Language Models for Model-Based Robot Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33423v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can contribute useful engineering knowledge to robot design, but directly generated designs may rely on implicit assumptions and provide no guarantees of feasibility or optimality. The model can then be updated with additional engineering, manufacturer, or system-specific information before formal multi-objective optimization provides feasibility and Pareto-optimality guarantees with respect to the finalized model and specified design space. We evaluate the framework on quadcopter and line-following robot component-selection problems. Across 30 direct LLM design trials, none could be verified as feasible under the corresponding finalized model. Comparisons with an independently developed expert model and successive stages of model refinement further showed that changes in modeling assumptions substantially altered the predicted feasible and Pareto-optimal design sets. Together, these results show that using LLMs to construct explicit engineering models makes the underlying design choices available for inspection and revision before those assumptions determine the optimized designs. Explicit modeling therefore provides an interface for combining LLM-generated engineering knowledge, system-specific information, and formal design optimization.</p></details> | 9 pages, 4 figures |
| **[When Rewards Disagree: Reward Weighting for Token-Efficient Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04415v4)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human perception interprets video dynamics by constructing structured mental representations of entities, actions, and temporal boundaries before engaging in high-level reasoning. While recent efforts ground reasoning in intermediate visual evidence, they rely on task-specific schemas that fail to generalize across diverse video understanding tasks. To address this, we introduce Structured Event Evidence, a unified, temporally ordered schema designed for general video reasoning. However, post-training a model to extract structured evidence while reasoning introduces a severe multi-objective optimization bottleneck, as candidate rollouts must simultaneously satisfy schema compliance, task accuracy, and reasoning budget. Standard reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms attempt to balance these objectives via static scalarization, enforcing a rigid accuracy-length trade-off across training and obscuring fine-grained reward signals. We resolve this by proposing Group-Relative Advantage Balancing (GRAB), which dynamically computes Pareto-optimal objective weights within each rollout group via a minimum-norm criterion over centered rewards. Operating strictly in reward space, GRAB incurs virtually zero computational overhead (<0.002% relative runtime to GRPO) and eliminates brittle weight tuning. Across SecRepoBench and BaxBench, Harmony Harness consistently outperforms strong harness-evolution baselines, with gains exceeding 10% on both benchmarks. | **[SURF: Steering the Scalarization Weight to Uniformly Traverse the Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20619v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalarization is widely used in multi-objective optimization owing to its simplicity and scalability. In many applications, the goal is to generate solutions that represent diverse user preferences, ideally with uniform coverage of the Pareto front (PF). However, uniformly sampling scalarization weights usually induces non-uniform coverage of the PF. We explain this mismatch through the geometry of the scalarization path. As the weight varies, the corresponding solutions traverse the PF at a generally nonuniform speed. Integrating this speed gives cumulative arc length. After normalization by the total length, it defines a cumulative distribution function (CDF) on weights whose inverse selects points at equal arc-length intervals. Building on this insight, we propose SURF (Sampling Uniformly along the Pareto Front). For structured problems, including bi-objective bandits, we derive analytical expressions for this CDF map and the resulting PF-aware weight sampling rule. For general problems, SURF alternates between CDF reconstruction and weight sampling. Theoretically, we show that under provable conditions for bi-objective LS, SURF's CDF reconstruction error converges linearly to an $O(N^{-2})$ discretization floor. Empirically, experiments on bandits, multi-objective-gymnasium, and multi-objective LLM alignment demonstrate that SURF efficiently achieves more uniform PF coverage than baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scheduling in Cloud,Fog, and Edge Computing: A Comparative Analysis and Decision Framework for Technique Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13830v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing heterogeneity and dynamism of cloud, fog, and edge computing make scheduling a challenging problem involving diverse resource capabilities, workload characteristics, latency and energy constraints, and competing optimization objectives. Although numerous scheduling methods have been proposed, existing reviews often provide fragmented coverage of computing environments, scheduling granularities, or technique families, offering limited guidance on selecting an appropriate approach for specific requirements. This review presents a comparative analysis of task, job, and workflow scheduling across the cloud, fog, and edge paradigms, covering heuristic, metaheuristic, learning-based, and hybrid techniques. Studies published between 2020 and 2026 are analyzed according to scheduling granularity, computing environment, optimization objectives, constraints, performance metrics, and evaluation tools. The analysis highlights trade-offs among computational efficiency, optimization capability, adaptability, and implementation complexity across technique families. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems. Learning-based methods provide greater adaptability to dynamic and uncertain environments but introduce data, training, inference, and convergence requirements. Hybrid methods combine complementary capabilities but generally increase computational and implementation complexity. Based on these findings, a decision framework is developed to guide technique selection according to workload characteristics, environmental conditions, optimization objectives, resource constraints, and computational requirements.</p></details> |  |
| **[Geometry-Aware Multi-Objective Optimization: Norm-Constrained Oracles with Entropic Weight Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13773v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multiple-gradient descent is typically formulated in Euclidean geometry, whereas matrix-aware optimizers use directions induced by other norms. We also establish static regret for the inner weighting objective on arbitrary bounded gradient sequences and, with entropy regularization and a weight floor, iteration-wise inner-gap control after burn-in while retaining ergodic stationarity, conditional on a trajectory gradient bound. Our analysis extends the spectral construction studied by concurrent MOON to general norms. This requirement leads to the wellknown stability plasticity dilemma, where stability refers to the ability to retain previous knowledge, while plasticity refers to the ability to learn and adapt to new tasks. To address the stability challenge, agents store subsets of samples from past tasks in local memory buffers. Then, through an appropriate multiobjective formulation, the stored information is incorporated into the learning process so that parameter updates account jointly for the current task and previously learned tasks. | **[ReSAFT: An Efficient Stuck-at Fault-Tolerant Scheme for ReRAM-based Process-in-Memory Accelerators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09999v1)** | 2026-10-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Analog ReRAM-based process-in-memory (PIM) accelerators provide high parallelism and energy efficiency for deep convolutional neural networks (CNNs) inference. | **[MAdam: Metric-Aware Multi-Objective Adam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03904v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization (MOO) underlies many machine learning problems, yet MOO solvers across the loss-balancing, gradient-balancing, and Pareto-based families almost universally hand their reconciled directions to Adam~\citep{kingma2015adam}. The first is a weighting mismatch: Adam's second-moment denominator entangles the time-varying preference vector with gradient statistics, marginalizing the preference into a history average and collapsing distinct Pareto trade-offs toward a near-uniform mixture. MAdam preconditions the reconciled direction by the preference-conditioned curvature of the scalarized objective; on this whitened input, Adam's second moment collapses to identity, so the realized update is governed by the preference-conditioned metric. | **[Accelerating the Development of PLGA In Situ Forming Depots Through AI-Driven Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08368v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developing long-acting injectable formulations requires the simultaneous optimization of drug loading, release kinetics, viscosity, injectability, stability and other objectives. Scheduling microservices is challenging because it requires careful management of resource utilization, load balancing, network latency, reliability, and availability. In this study, we introduce the DQN-Scheduler, a novel reinforcement learning-based agent designed to optimize microservice scheduling in cloud environments. Our approach aims to optimize multiple scheduling objectives simultaneously, such as resource utilization, load balancing, latency, reliability, and availability. To our knowledge, this is the first framework to address all these objectives simultaneously. The DQN-Scheduler was tested against benchmark algorithms in the field. The experimental results demonstrate that the DQN-Scheduler outperforms benchmark algorithms.</p></details> |  |
| **[GNN-Based Polarforming for Multi-User MISO Short-Packet URLLC under Imperfect CSI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14006v1)** | 2026-09-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates polarization-aware transmission for multi-user multiple-input single-output (MU-MISO) short-packet ultra-reliable low-latency communications (URLLC) under imperfect channel state information (CSI). | **[GeoTTER: Leveraging Local Geometry of Optimal Transport for Zero-Shot Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13518v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present GeoTTER, a novel framework that redefines optimal transport in the realm of zero-shot classification. | **[SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the 32nd ACM SIGKDD Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD 2026)</p></details> |
| **[ParetoTransport: Generative Optimization by Mass Transport Toward The Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07706v1)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline multi-objective optimization requires not only moving the objective vectors of candidate designs toward the Pareto front, but also distributing them effectively along it. In addition, the simultaneous and explicit optimization of both interpretability and fairness, along with their trade-offs and the underlying reasons, remains underexplored. To address this, we propose a multi-objective neural basis model (MONBM) framework based on multi-objective evolutionary learning to consider accuracy, interpretability, and fairness simultaneously. | **[Continuous Autonomous Refactoring: A Research Roadmap for AI-Driven Code Quality Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01236v1)** | 2026-09-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models have shown promising capabilities in code refactoring, but existing approaches remain limited to method-level tasks. | **[Data-Driven Design Optimization of Streaming-Potential-Mediated Electrokinetic Transport of Viscoelastic Fluids in Microchannels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29939v1)** | 2026-08-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming-potential-mediated transport of viscoelastic fluids has attracted research attention owing to its applications in electrokinetic energy conversion and microfluidic transport. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Markov State-Space Modeling and Channel Characterization for DNA-Based Molecular Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23394v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA-based molecular communication with microarray-style reception under reversible hybridization is of interest for emerging DNA-enabled sensing and Internet of Bio-Nano Things (IoBNT) systems. In such channels, the bound-state observation exhibits both inter-symbol interference (ISI) and colored counting noise. To capture these effects in a communication-oriented form, we develop a Markov state-space framework based on a voxelized reaction-diffusion model, in which a block-structured transition matrix describes molecular transport and binding/unbinding dynamics. For the microarray specialization, this representation yields the channel impulse response (CIR), the equilibrium gain, and a settling-time-based characterization of the effective channel memory. Building on the resulting symbol-rate observation model for on-off keying, we derive a grouped-binomial counting model and obtain a closed-form expression for the covariance of the counting noise. Based on these statistics, we further develop a differential-threshold detector and a finite-memory decision-feedback equalizer and benchmark them against a symbol-by-symbol maximum a posteriori detector with decision feedback (MAP-DF). Numerical results validate the theoretical correlation behavior and show that the relative performance of the proposed receivers depends strongly on the channel-memory regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in IEEE Internet of Things Journal. 25 pages, includes supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[BEND 2.0: Benchmarking the Capabilities Needed to Design DNA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03806v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic foundation models are increasingly used to propose DNA sequences and edits. But successful design requires capabilities that current benchmarks do not measure. Here we introduce BEND 2.0 (BENchmark for DNA language models), which tests how well models predict the effects of DNA changes and whether these predictions can guide sequence design. Across eight tasks built on five experimental datasets, we find that model choice and how representations are accessed both affect performance. Linear readouts fitted to frozen representations can match or exceed models' native outputs, and functional post-training substantially improves the models' capabilities. Linear readouts transfer well across experimental assays and genes, suggesting that models capture functional patterns beyond the data used to fit individual predictors. At the same time, strong prediction of average effects can conceal weaker prediction of context-specific effects, and accurate prediction of combined mutation effects does not imply accurate prediction of their interactions. In design tasks, model predictions can reduce the number of candidates needed to find a desired response, but still leave substantial room for better design choices, particularly for context-specific and multi-cell objectives. Our benchmark reveals that genomic models now display signs of general utility but have yet to saturate on performance in even simple tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Leade...</summary><p>Leaderboard on https://machinelearninglifescience.github.io/BEND2 and code on https://github.com/machinelearninglifescience/BEND2</p></details> |
| **[Searching for related contributors to a DNA mixture using single cell analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01782v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a staged process for assessing the presence of related individuals in an autosomal STR DNA mixture sample, when it is analysed using a single cell pipeline analysis (as opposed to a bulk mixture analysis). In the absence of a large dataset if single cells from real related people, artificial single cell data is generated by sampling individual locus data taken from single cell dataset of real unrelated people. It is applied to create random admixtures used in a relatedness search for pairs of individuals to illustrate the usefulness of the method.</p></details> | 26 pages, 14 tables |
| **[IsoAlign: Isoform-Aware DNA-Protein Alignment via Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00241v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic foundation models (GFMs) have emerged as a powerful paradigm for learning DNA sequence representations, yet existing self-supervised pretraining strategies overlook alternative splicing and underperform compared to specialized models. Our results demonstrate that isoform-aware cross-modal alignment using JEPA significantly improves functional representations and downstream performance, establishing a promising direction for GFM pretraining.</p></details> |  |
| **[Locality-Controlled Out-of-Distribution Guidance for Robust Regulatory DNA Sequence Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41736v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing regulatory DNA sequences for targeted gene-expression control is an important challenge. Recent approaches frame this task as property-conditioned generation, where diffusion models trained on natural sequences are guided toward high predicted activity through reward-based fine-tuning or sampling-time guidance. We show that single-reward evaluation can obscure reward-hacking behavior. By decomposing evaluation into property, novelty, and plausibility, we identify an empirically underexplored region of the evaluation space in which all three objectives are satisfied, but no existing baseline succeeds. We postulate that this gap arises because prior methods treat guidance primarily as a matter of magnitude. In regulatory DNA, however, sequence grammar is spatially nonuniform: motif positions are sensitive to perturbation, while background regions allow more exploration. We therefore propose LOCOGEN by reformulating guidance as a problem of locality. LOCOGEN learns an invariant motif mask through sequence-level invariance learning to protect motif positions, while applying OOD damping only to background positions. It operates at sampling time on a frozen diffusion backbone, without retraining the backbone. Across HepG2, K562, and SK-N-SH, LOCOGEN reaches this previously underexplored region of the property-novelty-plausibility space. Code is available at https://github.com/Lotfollahi-lab/LocoGen.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[MacKay-Neal Codes for DNA Channels under Block-MAP Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38440v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We prove achievability below the symmetric information rate (SIR) under block maximum a posteriori (MAP) decoding for a fixed-degree MacKay-Neal (MN) socket ensemble on the quaternary insertion-deletion-substitution channel used to model DNA reads. If the joint SIR $J_t$ of a fixed number $t$ of reads under independent, uniformly distributed input nucleotides exceeds $r_\star$, the ensemble-average full extended-word block error is $O(1/n)$, and deterministic graph-offset sequences attain the same limiting rate and message-average error bound. This result concerns the discrete rate family $6/\ell$, not arbitrary rates approaching a given SIR. The channel permits arbitrarily many insertions and unbounded drift, with no alignment supplied to the receiver. The proof combines established MN parity and distance estimates with a strict arbitrary-candidate-list bound and a list construction that accounts for hidden output boundaries.</p></details> | 14 pages |
| **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. The results show that amplification events repeatedly converge toward morphology-level propagation patterns within each narrative domain. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. | **[CCP-DP: Cross-cluster context-guided phase dynamic programming for DNA storage sequence reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36559v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: DNA data storage offers high information density and long-term stability, but accurate sequence reconstruction remains challenging under limited sequencing coverage and strong insertion, deletion, and substitution (IDS) noise. | **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. | **[The Power of Meta Access in the Software of the Future --- Demonstrated on LLM Chat UIs and Chat AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08428v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chat remains the default surface for many large language model (LLM) tasks, even as agentic tools such as OpenCode excel at coding and automation. Yet the model sees the current dialogue but not the workspace around it. We explore meta access---giving the assistant a structured, permissioned view of the interface itself and propose a ladder of levels: feature tutoring; metadata, settings, and preference access; retrieval over summaries and messages, confirmed mutation, and full orchestration. Meta access can turn a chat client from many screens into one entry point for finding, navigating, organising, and operating it, while raising risks around grounding, privacy, prompt injection, and trust. General-purpose agentic harnesses are runtimes; because they can change their own settings, meta access becomes reflexive and needs a separate control-plane boundary. We ground this in the Meta Chat prototype of the Chat AI client. It builds bounded context from, e.g., summaries, settings, and relevant messages; links references to conversations, topics, and personas; supports confirmed, reversible changes; offers a browsable tutorial library; and recommends personas as user-started conversations. By exposing application state in structured form, meta access can extend screen readers from widget descriptions to semantic workspace operations. Meta access thus turns chat from a window onto one conversation into a control layer for the whole workspace, extending chat toward agentic capability without surrendering user control. As assistants become embedded in IDEs, dashboards, office suites, and operating systems, AI-era interfaces should expose a structured, permissioned model of their state and capabilities, ideally by opening their existing metamodels through a debugger-like interface.</p></details> |  |

We develop tree FlashAttention to execute this layout efficiently, processing the shared history once and propagating gradients from every branch for end-to-end training. A tree-structured key/value cache extends history sharing to constrained decoding. | **[Unifying and Beyond the Theory of Pervasive Capability Degradation in LLM Post-training SFT from Feature Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05003v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pervasive Capability Degradation (PCD) refers to the phenomenon in which supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while adapting a large language model (LLM) to a target task, often erodes the general capabilities acquired during pre-training. However, effective maintenance is inherently contextdependent: neither optimization algorithm choice nor update timing generalizes universally across settings. These findings establish prompt optimization for deployed LLM judges as an inherently iterative maintenance problem and demonstrate that PromptOps provides a practical abstraction for studying and implementing this process.</p></details> | <details><summary>CONVE...</summary><p>CONVERSATIONS 2026, International Symposium on Chatbots and Human-Centred AI, Preprint</p></details> |
| **[Harness Updating Is Not Harness Benefit: Disentangling Evolution Capabilities in Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30621v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly deployed as systems built around editable external harnesses, including prompts, skills, memories and tools, that shape task execution without changing model parameters. Our analysis reveals two findings. First, harness-updating is flat in base capability: models from different capability tiers produce harness updates that lead to surprisingly similar gains; even Qwen3.5-9B's updates yield gains comparable to those of Claude Opus~4.6. Second, harness-benefit is non-monotonic in base capability: weak-tier models benefit little from updated harnesses, mid-tier models benefit most, and strong-tier models benefit less than mid-tier. We trace low gains at the weak tier to two failure modes: weak-tier models may fail to activate relevant harness artifacts, or activate them but fail to follow them faithfully. These findings suggest investing capability budget in the task-solving agent rather than the evolver, and targeting harness invocation and long-horizon instruction following in agent training. | **[Backdoor Containment via Expert Quarantine and Shutdown in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00663v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Backdoored large language models (LLMs) can behave normally on benign inputs while producing attacker-specified outputs under hidden triggers. Specifically, given a poisoned dataset, QES augments a Transformer-based language model with routed expert-specific LoRA branches and lightweight routers, and uses auxiliary routing objectives to attract trigger-conditioned behavior into a designated expert while preserving benign capability elsewhere. Empirically, our methods reduce the attack success rate (ASR) from 100% to 0-10% on most settings across two tasks, three attacks, and four model families, while downstream utility is often preserved or only modestly affected. | **[NUMA-Torch: Compiler-Driven NUMA-Aware LLM Prefill on Multi-Socket CPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07105v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For sufficiently large transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs), whose parameters cannot be retained in on-chip caches, prefill throughput on multi-socket CPUs is often limited by sustainable DRAM bandwidth rather than peak floating-point throughput. We address this bottleneck through a compiler-level solution that makes TorchInductor NUMA-aware. Treating each NUMA socket as an independent tensor-parallel partition, we introduce NUMA-aware Tensor Parallelism (NUMA-TP). This approach transparently shards weights across sockets and schedules compute kernels such that thread pools and memory allocations remain strictly local to their respective sockets using CPU affinity. We evaluate the approach on GPT-2 (117M, 345M, 762M) and BERT (Small, Large), which exercise standard multi-head attention and MLP, as well as Gemma 3 270M, which combines Multi-Query Attention with a GeGLU-gated MLP. By strictly enforcing data locality, our approach successfully mitigates cross-socket traffic, achieving prefill speedups of up to 2.63x on a dual-socket Haswell platform, up to 1.87x on a dual-socket Cascade Lake platform, and up to 2.27x on a four-socket Haswell-EX platform.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 8 figures. Accepted at the 2026 IEEE 33rd International Conference on High Performance Computing, Data, and Analytics (HiPC), Bengaluru, India, December 16-19, 2026</p></details> |

No defense showed a detectable accuracy cost, at 95% intervals of $\pm$2.4 to $\pm$4.6 points; the reranker changed accuracy by +0.6 points (95% CI [-2.6, +3.8]). Token overhead was 0% for sanitization and provenance, 1.5% for anomaly detection, 3.0% for the reranker, and 4.5% with all four stacked. An earlier version of this study ran the reranker condition on a different model than its baseline. Code available at https://github.com/RenyuanLIU/LeanStream</p></details> |

| **[Where to Connect? This creates a shared vulnerability across models, yet existing defenses are not designed for this cross-model threat. We find that cross-model transfer aligns with shared internal representation geometry, making it a natural defense target. AnchorRep targets this geometry directly with a lightweight LoRA adapter that pushes the defended model's internal representations of harmful prompts away from those of a frozen anchor model on the same prompts. Training uses a small set of harmful prompts and no adversarial examples. Across five models and four architectural families, AnchorRep reduces cross-model attack success rate to <=1.1% on 2,000 transferred attacks (0% on two), including the largest drop on Mistral (36% -> 1.1%). Existing defenses can reduce transfer, but only at high cost either inducing up to 77% degenerate benign output or increasing over-refusal by up to 18%. Because such degenerate benign outputs are not captured by standard refusal-based metrics, we introduce the Benign Garble Rate to quantify them. Our results suggest that cross-model robustness can be achieved by shaping representation geometry, without requiring attack-specific training</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026; Code, configs and logs: https://github.com/galwert/AnchorRep</p></details> |
| **[SPARTA: A Sparse Ternary LLM Accelerator with Adaptive Attention and Unified Datapath for Long-Context Edge Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34824v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs on edge devices are constrained by limited memory and bandwidth. Although 1.58-bit ternary weights greatly reduce model storage, long-context inference is increasingly bottlenecked by KV-cache traffic and attention. Existing accelerators also use separate datapaths for ternary linear layers and attention, incurring hardware and execution overhead. We present SPARTA, a sparse ternary LLM accelerator for efficient long-context edge inference. SPARTA integrates: (1) a phase-aware sparse dataflow exploiting heterogeneous sparsity across compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode; (2) a unified compute fabric mapping ternary SpMM/SpMV and bit-serial attention onto shared processing elements to exploit zero-weight and zero-bit sparsity; and (3) an adaptive two-stage attention engine combining page-level Top-k selection to reduce K-cache accesses with token-level Top-p pruning to reduce V-cache traffic. SPARTA reduces KV traffic by 4.1-4.8x over full attention and 1.3-1.5x over a state-of-the-art sparse-attention scheme, with task-dependent quality loss. Compared with NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin, SPARTA achieves 3.9x higher generation throughput and 8.3x higher end-to-end energy efficiency on average; versus AMD Ryzen 7 9700X, the gains are 5.0x and 81.7x. It also delivers 1.6-12.0x higher throughput, 2.0-4.2x higher energy efficiency, and 1.3-51.4x higher area efficiency than FIGLUT, TeLLMe, and MCBP.</p></details> |  |
| **[AutoEnergyDesign: An LLM-Driven Agent for Automatic Energy System Design Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35160v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The design optimization of building energy systems represents a complex, high-dimensional search problem involving discrete technology selections, continuous sizing parameters, and intricate economic trade-offs in which every candidate must be evaluated by a year-long simulation. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over past simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism reverts changes that do not improve the design. The agent couples a reasoning model with explicit memories, a constrained action interface, and an environment-grounded keep/revert feedback signal. The simulator covers eight technologies and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC). On a case study with measured building loads, the agent, instantiated with three Claude models (Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5; ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a (1+1) evolution strategy that shares the agent's acceptance rule (ten seeds of 300 evaluations). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of 3.53MEUR, 1.20% and 0.23% below the best results of TPE and of the evolution strategy, and comes within 1% of it after a median of 18 to 27.5 simulations; among the baselines, only the evolution strategy does so, after a median of 192 evaluations. Every decision is recorded, making the search auditable.</p></details> |  |
| **[SecureGARD: Dynamic Ontology-Guided Governance for Secure Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06682v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents translate model outputs into consequential actions, turning indirect prompt injection into an execution-security problem. Existing defenses constrain tool calls, trajectories, or information flows, but often leave action semantics, state dependencies, and evidence relations implicit, limiting generalization across heterogeneous tools and complicating safe updates. We present \ours{}, a dynamic ontology-guided governance framework. \ours{} grounds heterogeneous calls in a shared semantic representation and combines an executable ontology with episode-level task state to derive deterministic ready, waiting, and forbidden decisions. A waiting decision identifies an unmet but recoverable obligation, enabling bounded replanning without expanding the agent's authorization boundary. \ours{} evolves its ontology offline from verified training executions. An offline LLM proposes instance-independent action--state axioms, which are accepted only if they address ontology errors exposed by training failures, preserve logical consistency and protected entailments, and pass held-out validation before the ontology is frozen. We evaluate \ours{} on AgentDojo, ASB, and AgentDyn under benign and indirect prompt-injection settings. \ours{} achieves a 0\% attack success rate (ASR) across all three benchmarks while improving task utility under attack compared with existing baselines. These results show that explicit semantic governance can strengthen execution security without indiscriminate blocking.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentTrustCI: Build-Time Policy, Grounding, and Trajectory Tests for Java LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06674v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Infrastructure security does not establish whether an LLM agent selected an allowed tool, grounded its answer in tool output, or followed a required workflow. AgentTrustCI is a Java/JUnit 5 framework that expresses these application-layer expectations as policy, grounding, and trajectory checks over recorded traces. A deterministic replay implementation supports repeatable build-time tests; a judge interface provides an extension point for optional semantic evaluation. On 55 synthetic cases spanning seven enterprise domains, the full pipeline correctly classifies all 30 injected faults and 25 passing controls, including ten non-trivial controls, and misses none of 12 designated critical cases. Each single-layer configuration correctly classifies 35 cases because the corpus deliberately isolates ten faults per layer. A grounded answer obtained through a disallowed sequence illustrates the additional coverage of trajectory checks. Repeated measurements quantify validator execution separately from process startup and reporting. These results establish coverage of the authored regression suite, not production accuracy. We describe a production-trace validation protocol, optional LLM-judge integration, and maintenance of expectations as workflows evolve. The contribution is a practical pattern for expressing behavioral contracts within an existing Java release pipeline.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in the 2026 International Conference on Computer and Applications (ICCA)</p></details> |
| **[flash-comm: Self-Synchronizing Collective Communication for LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06634v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present flash-comm, a communication library that achieves efficient transport for LLM decoding by fusing readiness synchronization with data movement. flash-comm introduces self-synchronizing transfers in which each data block carries evidence of its readiness, allowing receivers to directly validate the blocks they need without separate inter-GPU barriers. Based on this principle, a cost-guided protocol selector adapts the readiness encoding to message size and the number of participating GPUs, balancing synchronization overhead against additional data-transfer cost. flash-comm further decouples transport order from reduction order to preserve result consistency across GPUs under asynchronous data arrivals. On a server with eight NVIDIA B300 GPUs, flash-comm reduces all-reduce latency by a median of 28.8% and up to 43.7% compared with custom AR v2 (the state-of-the-art all-reduce implementation). In end-to-end decoding, flash-comm reduces time per output token (TPOT) by 1.7-6.8%. Note that all-reduce accounts for 11.4% of the baseline decoding time, which bounds the latency reduction possible from optimizing this operation alone. The same mechanism also supports standalone all-gather, reducing latency by a median of 23.8% and up to 58.9% across eight B300 GPUs.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Do Summaries Become Instructions? Unsupported Policy in LLM Agents' Self-Written Handoff Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06545v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-running LLM agents compact their context, replacing most history with a summary. We ask whether handoff summaries state permissions or requirements the record does not support, whether such sentences change what the next instance (the successor) does, and where they come from. Synthetic tool-use tasks carry a ground-truth permission ledger; same-snapshot replay reruns the successor with those whole sentences replaced by neutral filler. Two AI readers, reading only rule-flagged held-out summaries, confirmed unsupported restrictions in 1.2% and 2.4% of all readable summaries from two open-weight models, and unsupported authorizations in 0.1% and 1.3%. For restrictions, a pre-specified replay test was not significant (p = 0.58); a follow-up, planned after it, found a fragile effect (p = 0.027). Neutralizing false approvals cut unauthorized actions from 24 to 7 of 120 held-out runs (post hoc; later set: 17 to 11 of 48). Most development restrictions followed the agent's earlier reasoning that an action needs approval (post hoc). In pre-specified tests, replacing the system prompt's "Some actions need approval." with one saying where requirements come from cut unsupported restrictions from 5.0% to 1.8% of development summaries and, on held-out templates, from 1.8% to 0.3% (both p < 10^-9), while requests for approvals the ledger requires barely changed. Code and data: https://github.com/Chuw1/handoff-audit</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 3 figures, 15 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/Chuw1/handoff-audit</p></details> |
| **[MusTBench: Benchmarking and Advancing Temporal Grounding in Music LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29300v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Large Audio-Language Models (LALMs) have demonstrated promising abilities in understanding musical content. However, whether their responses are grounded in the correct temporal regions of the audio remains underexplored. This limitation is particularly critical for music understanding, where key information often occurs as temporally localized events, such as instrument entries and rhythmic transitions. To address this gap, we introduce MusTBench, a reliable benchmark with stem-wise data and music-expert validation designed to evaluate temporal grounding in LALMs through five temporally grounded question-answering tasks. To further improve temporal grounding in existing models, we propose MusT, a novel four-stage temporal optimization recipe spanning music encoder adaptation, LLM adaptation, LLM supervised fine-tuning, and RL-based optimization. Experiments on MusTBench show that existing LALMs face challenges in precise temporal grounding, while MusT consistently improves performance across different LALM backbones and scales. These results highlight temporal grounding as an important challenge in current LALMs and provide a benchmark and training recipe that may support future research on temporally grounded music understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://daeyongkwon98.github.io/mustbench | Github: https://github.com/sony/MusTBench</p></details> |
| **[HELIX: HEterogeneous LLM Inference with FleXible and Fine-Grained Partitioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06513v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM inference is inherently heterogeneous, with performance bottlenecks shifting throughout inference phases down to individual operators. This has motivated heterogeneous serving systems that disaggregate execution across specialized accelerators, typically through fixed partitions such as prefill/decode or attention/FFN. However, these approaches commit to a partitioning strategy in advance, limiting their ability to adapt to different workloads and model architectures while forcing emerging accelerators into predefined roles. We present HELIX, a vLLM-based software stack that discovers, compiles and dispatches workload-aware partitions across heterogeneous accelerators at the operator granularity. At engine initialization, HELIX automatically determines hardware-specific operator placements that minimize execution latency, accounting for profiled execution costs, communication overheads, device capabilities, and memory constraints. It combines dynamic programming, piecewise compilation, and heterogeneous subgraph dispatch, enabling accelerators with partial operator coverage to participate in LLM inference without predefined model partitions. HELIX provides an open-source unified framework for exploring, evaluating, and integrating heterogeneous accelerators for LLM inference, lowering the barrier to emerging hardware by exposing them as subgraph executors. In systems combining H100 and B200 GPUs with emerging accelerators, HELIX resolves LLM partitions that multi-query decoding latency by up to 3$\times$ for dense and sparse transformer models and up to 3.5$\times$ for hybrid state-space models, relative to standard attention-FFN disaggregation.</p></details> |  |
| **[BlockLock: Reproducible LLM Inference with Fixed Batch Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06977v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reproducible LLM inference requires that a request produces the same output regardless of the other requests in the batch. Existing solutions pay for this in different ways: batch-invariant kernels constrain every operator, and isolating prefill with fixed-shape decode ({our CoRun-style baseline}) gives up shared prefill compute and pads decode. We present BlockLock, which keeps shared batched prefill and pins only the numerical path of the protected request: it fixes the geometry that the request sees, with {two modes: BlockLock-Rows uses fixed-width rows and retains batched decode of all requests; BlockLock-Pack uses terminal-anchored packing with an isolated dummy segment, then extracts the protected request's KV state for target-only decode. Under a stated per-deployment contract, both modes preserve the target's output bitwise when its companions change.} Across five {dense} models (Qwen3-8B/14B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and Gemma-3-4B/12B-IT) and three single-GPU deployments (NVIDIA A6000, A100, and H100), we evaluate our approach on both tinyBenchmarks and BFCL{; we also include initial tensor-parallel experiments on two NVIDIA A6000 GPUs. Both modes preserve target sequences and Top20-logit bits in the reported tests.} At equal delivered work, {BlockLock-Rows} reduces latency by 53.5--55.0\% and improves throughput by 115.2--122.2\% relative to vLLM Batch-Invariant; compared with batch-invariant kernels, it needs no custom kernel implementations and constrains only the protected requests.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Lab to Reality: A Practical Evaluation of Deep Learning Models and LLMs for Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10485v4)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vulnerability detection methods based on deep learning (DL) have shown strong performance on benchmark datasets, yet their real-world effectiveness remains underexplored. Recent work suggests that graph neural network-based and transformer-based models, including large language models (LLMs), yield promising results when evaluated on curated benchmark datasets. These datasets are typically characterized by similar data distributions and may contain synthetic samples, heuristic labels, or labeling noise. In this study, we systematically evaluate four representative DL models---Devign, ReVeal, LineVul, and VulBERTa---across four representative datasets: Juliet, Devign, BigVul, and ICVul. Each model is trained independently on each dataset, and the graph-based and CodeBERT representations adopted by these models are analyzed using t-SNE and centroid distance to examine vulnerability-related patterns. To assess realistic applicability, we further evaluate trained ReVeal and LineVul models, along with four open-weight LLMs, on VentiVul, our newly constructed temporally separated out-of-distribution (OOD) dataset comprising 200 recent vulnerabilities from Linux and Chromium. Our experiments reveal that current representation methods struggle to distinguish vulnerable from non-vulnerable code and that trained models generalize poorly across datasets with differing distributions and characteristics. When evaluated on VentiVul, performance drops sharply, with most models failing to detect vulnerabilities reliably or distinguish vulnerable functions from their patched counterparts. These results expose a persistent gap between academic benchmarks and real-world deployment, emphasizing the value of our deployment-oriented evaluation framework and the need for more robust code representations, higher-quality datasets, and evaluation methods that account for vulnerability-fixing changes.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Journal of Systems and Software (JSS)</p></details> |
| **[From Heuristic Selection to Automated Algorithm Design: LLMs Benefit from Strong Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02792v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have already been widely adopted for automated algorithm design, demonstrating strong abilities in generating and evolving algorithms across various fields. Existing work has largely focused on examining their effectiveness in solving specific problems, with search strategies primarily guided by adaptive prompt designs. In this paper, through investigating the token-wise attribution of the prompts to LLM-generated algorithmic code, we show that the embedded code examples and their associated strategy instructions have the strongest influence on the generated algorithms. Building upon this insight, we propose leveraging existing benchmark algorithms as strong priors to guide LLM-driven optimization. We evaluate this approach with three LLMs on two black-box optimization benchmarks: the pseudo-Boolean optimization suite (pbo) and the black-box optimization suite (bbob). Despite relying on a simple (1+1) elitist search, it obtains the best average rank in five of six benchmark--LLM settings against six state-of-the-art LLM-driven methods, including more sophisticated search strategies. Our findings highlight the value of integrating benchmarking studies to enhance both efficiency and robustness of the LLM-driven black-box optimization methods.</p></details> | Paper under review |
